People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi Doctor. I guess what brings me in is... I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting really bad at the shop. Like, I’ll be in the middle of a project, and just one saw starting up in the background—we mostly use these Makita miter saws, though my buddy Dave swears by DeWalt, and he's always trying to get me to switch. I've had mine for ten years, you know, and it's never let me down. But anyway... if a saw starts up, or if somebody just walks past the office, or even if I just have a random thought about another job I need to do, it completely pulls me off what I’m doing. And then the whole day just gets away from me. Just yesterday I wasted hours because I thought about Mrs. Gable's cabinets, and she's a sweet lady, makes the absolute best snickerdoodles, but I wasn't even supposed to be working on her kitchen yet! I just... I can't stay on track.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I've always been a little all over the place, I guess, but this specific level of trouble? That's definitely newer. It really ramped up about a year and a month ago. That was right around the time I broke my wrist. 

Gosh, that was a whole ordeal. I was unloading some beautiful cherry wood—I was actually going to build a custom dining table for my niece's wedding. She had this gorgeous outdoor ceremony up in Colorado, though the altitude really did a number on my breathing. I had to sit down twice just walking to the reception tent! But anyway, I slipped on some sawdust in the shop and caught myself completely wrong. Snapped it right there. 

So because I was in this bulky cast, they had to shift me off the tools. They put me on shop paperwork and supervising the younger crew instead of my usual hands-on carpentry. And let me tell you, sitting at a desk looking at invoices... I don't know how office people do it. My chair squeaks every single time I move, and I keep meaning to bring in some WD-40. I actually bought a can at the hardware store last Tuesday, but I keep leaving it in the cup holder of my truck... 

Wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The focus. Yeah, since that switch to the paperwork a year and a month ago, my brain just will not stay put.

But anyway... if a saw starts up, or if somebody just walks past the office, or even if I just have a random thought about another job I need to do, it completely pulls me off what I’m doing. And then the whole day just gets away from me. Just yesterday I wasted hours because I thought about Mrs. Gable's cabinets, and she's a sweet lady, makes the absolute best snickerdoodles, but I wasn't even supposed to be working on her kitchen yet! I just... I can't stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing like this, or is this something that's gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a little all over the place, I guess, but this specific level of trouble? That's definitely newer. It really ramped up about a year and a month ago. That was right around the time I broke my wrist. \n\nGosh, that was a whole ordeal. I was unloading some beautiful cherry wood—I was actually going to build a custom dining table for my niece's wedding. She had this gorgeous outdoor ceremony up in Colorado, though the altitude really did a number on my breathing. I had to sit down twice just walking to the reception tent! But anyway, I slipped on some sawdust in the shop and caught myself completely wrong. Snapped it right there. \n\nSo because I was in this bulky cast, they had to shift me off the tools. They put me on shop paperwork and supervising the younger crew instead of my usual hands-on carpentry. And let me tell you, sitting at a desk looking at invoices... I don't know how office people do it. My chair squeaks every single time I move, and I keep meaning to bring in some WD-40. I actually bought a can at the hardware store last Tuesday, but I keep leaving it in the cup holder of my truck... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The focus. Yeah, since that switch to the paperwork a year and a month ago, my brain just will not stay put.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Poor little orange thing, looks just like a cat I had when I was a kid named Marmalade. Marmalade used to sleep right on top of my dad's old Chevy... \n\nBut no, I'm not sitting at the desk worrying about things going wrong. It's more like my brain just... empties out, or fills up with a hundred different things that aren't the invoices. I'll be looking at a lumber order, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to water the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about how the neighbor's dog keeps digging under the fence, and before I know it, I'm looking up fence repair videos on YouTube. But I'm not anxious about it, you know? Just... completely sidetracked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, absolutely. If I have to sit at that desk doing estimates, or if they drag me into one of those long safety meetings... I just feel so wound up inside. Like I physically need to get up and move around. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. 

It reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... 

But yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.

Just... completely sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that desk trying to work on the invoices, do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety—like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel like your body is just itching to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. 

Neck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... 

But yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.

He'll pace the kitchen floor for an hour if the electric bill is ten dollars higher than last month. I keep telling him it's just the space heater I put out in the garage for the stray cat that's been hanging around. Poor little orange thing, looks just like a cat I had when I was a kid named Marmalade. Marmalade used to sleep right on top of my dad's old Chevy... \n\nBut no, I'm not sitting at the desk worrying about things going wrong. It's more like my brain just... empties out, or fills up with a hundred different things that aren't the invoices. I'll be looking at a lumber order, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to water the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about how the neighbor's dog keeps digging under the fence, and before I know it, I'm looking up fence repair videos on YouTube. But I'm not anxious about it, you know? Just... completely sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that desk trying to work on the invoices, do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety—like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel like your body is just itching to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. If I have to sit at that desk doing estimates, or if they drag me into one of those long safety meetings... I just feel so wound up inside. Like I physically need to get up and move around. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\nDoctor: When you broke your wrist in that fall on the sawdust, did you hit your head at all, or did you have any other injuries besides the wrist?\n\nPatient: No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. 

It's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... 

But yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.

Like, I’ll be in the middle of a project, and just one saw starting up in the background—we mostly use these Makita miter saws, though my buddy Dave swears by DeWalt, and he's always trying to get me to switch. I've had mine for ten years, you know, and it's never let me down. But anyway... if a saw starts up, or if somebody just walks past the office, or even if I just have a random thought about another job I need to do, it completely pulls me off what I’m doing. And then the whole day just gets away from me. Just yesterday I wasted hours because I thought about Mrs. Gable's cabinets, and she's a sweet lady, makes the absolute best snickerdoodles, but I wasn't even supposed to be working on her kitchen yet! I just... I can't stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing like this, or is this something that's gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a little all over the place, I guess, but this specific level of trouble? That's definitely newer. It really ramped up about a year and a month ago. That was right around the time I broke my wrist. \n\nGosh, that was a whole ordeal. I was unloading some beautiful cherry wood—I was actually going to build a custom dining table for my niece's wedding. She had this gorgeous outdoor ceremony up in Colorado, though the altitude really did a number on my breathing. I had to sit down twice just walking to the reception tent! But anyway, I slipped on some sawdust in the shop and caught myself completely wrong. Snapped it right there. \n\nSo because I was in this bulky cast, they had to shift me off the tools. They put me on shop paperwork and supervising the younger crew instead of my usual hands-on carpentry. And let me tell you, sitting at a desk looking at invoices... I don't know how office people do it. My chair squeaks every single time I move, and I keep meaning to bring in some WD-40. I actually bought a can at the hardware store last Tuesday, but I keep leaving it in the cup holder of my truck... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The focus. Yeah, since that switch to the paperwork a year and a month ago, my brain just will not stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble focusing on your work, do you find yourself worrying a lot—like about things that might go wrong, or tasks you need to get done, or other concerns?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the roof caving in or whether the bank is going to call. My husband, Tom, now he's a worrier. He'll pace the kitchen floor for an hour if the electric bill is ten dollars higher than last month. I keep telling him it's just the space heater I put out in the garage for the stray cat that's been hanging around. Poor little orange thing, looks just like a cat I had when I was a kid named Marmalade. Marmalade used to sleep right on top of my dad's old Chevy... \n\nBut no, I'm not sitting at the desk worrying about things going wrong. It's more like my brain just... empties out, or fills up with a hundred different things that aren't the invoices. I'll be looking at a lumber order, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to water the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about how the neighbor's dog keeps digging under the fence, and before I know it, I'm looking up fence repair videos on YouTube. But I'm not anxious about it, you know? Just... completely sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that desk trying to work on the invoices, do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety—like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel like your body is just itching to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. If I have to sit at that desk doing estimates, or if they drag me into one of those long safety meetings... I just feel so wound up inside. Like I physically need to get up and move around. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\nDoctor: When you broke your wrist in that fall on the sawdust, did you hit your head at all, or did you have any other injuries besides the wrist?\n\nPatient: No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you fell on the sawdust and broke your wrist, did you lose consciousness at any point, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. \n\nIt's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... \n\nBut yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, even way back in grade school, well before I was 12, my teachers were always telling my mother the same thing. They'd say I was bright, but just so careless. I was always losing my homework, or I'd forget the instructions right after they gave them, and I'd just sit there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. 

I remember in Mrs. Gable's fourth-grade class, my desk was right next to this big oak tree outside the window. We had these huge windows in that old school building—they don't make them like that anymore, all brick and those tall windows that you had to use a long wooden pole with a hook on the end just to pull the top sash down. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used to let me help him with the pole sometimes if I stayed after school. He was such a sweet older man, always had a pocket full of those strawberry candies with the wrappers that looked like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even still make them. Tom's mom used to keep a glass dish of them on her coffee table, right next to this awful porcelain poodle collection... 

But yeah, the attention stuff has been there my whole life. The wrist thing just made it so I have to sit at a desk more, which is why it's driving me so crazy now.

Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\nDoctor: When you broke your wrist in that fall on the sawdust, did you hit your head at all, or did you have any other injuries besides the wrist?\n\nPatient: No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you fell on the sawdust and broke your wrist, did you lose consciousness at any point, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. \n\nIt's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... \n\nBut yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school as a child and teenager, did you have trouble paying attention in class or staying focused on your schoolwork back then, or is this attention problem something that really only started around the time you broke your wrist?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, even way back in grade school, well before I was 12, my teachers were always telling my mother the same thing. They'd say I was bright, but just so careless. I was always losing my homework, or I'd forget the instructions right after they gave them, and I'd just sit there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. \n\nI remember in Mrs. Gable's fourth-grade class, my desk was right next to this big oak tree outside the window. We had these huge windows in that old school building—they don't make them like that anymore, all brick and those tall windows that you had to use a long wooden pole with a hook on the end just to pull the top sash down. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used to let me help him with the pole sometimes if I stayed after school. He was such a sweet older man, always had a pocket full of those strawberry candies with the wrappers that looked like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even still make them. Tom's mom used to keep a glass dish of them on her coffee table, right next to this awful porcelain poodle collection... \n\nBut yeah, the attention stuff has been there my whole life. The wrist thing just made it so I have to sit at a desk more, which is why it's driving me so crazy now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I never actually failed a grade, thank goodness. I mean, I scraped by. Barely passed some of them, especially math. I was always in the lowest math group, the one they put in the back corner of the room. We had these workbooks with the green covers, and I swear, just looking at that green color made me want to take a nap. 

Green has always been a weird color for me anyway. Like, I love the color of grass and trees, obviously, but green clothes? Never look right on me. It washes out my complexion completely. My sister, though, she can wear emerald green and look like a movie star. She wore this stunning green velvet dress to our cousin's wedding back in... oh, it must have been '88 or '89, because they had that terrible DJ who kept playing Richard Marx songs. She spilled red wine down the front of it during the toast. Red wine on green velvet—it was a disaster. She tried using club soda and salt, which is what my grandmother always swore by, but it just left this weird crusty ring. Grandma had all these home remedies. She used to put butter on burns, which I guess they say now is the worst thing you can do...

But anyway, no, I didn't get held back. I just got a lot of C-minuses and notes sent home about needing to "apply myself" more.

He'll pace the kitchen floor for an hour if the electric bill is ten dollars higher than last month. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. the wrong measurements or part numbers on estimates, like writing 36 instead of 63 or missing a line on the permit form, and then I only catch it after the lumber is ordered or somebody has to redo the work.\n- key: S003\n  name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: When a client or inspector is talking right to me, I’ll be looking at them and nodding, but then I realize I missed half of what they said and have to ask them to go over it again like I wasn’t even there.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: Since I’ve been stuck doing the shop paperwork, I can’t seem to figure out what needs doing first, so bids, schedules, invoices, and apprentice questions all pile up on my bench until I’m rushing and still missing deadlines.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for my keys, tape measure, phone, order sheets, or the notes I just had in my hand, and it eats up so much of the day that the guys have started checking behind me.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore—one saw starting up, somebody walking past the office, or even a thought about another job will pull me off what I’m doing, and then the whole day gets away from me.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit at the desk doing estimates or sit through a long meeting, I feel wound up inside like I need to get up and move around, even though my wrist is healed and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even at home or in church, when things are supposed to be quiet, I catch myself tapping, shifting around, whispering to my husband, or getting up for no real reason because sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, before I was 12, teachers were always telling my mother I was bright but careless, losing homework, forgetting instructions, and staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My mistakes and delays have cost me commissions, caused rework on jobs, and made the crew less willing to rely on me for scheduling or ordering. At home, my husband has had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about a year and a month, since I broke my wrist and got shifted into shop paperwork and supervising instead of hands-on carpentry.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you fell on the sawdust and broke your wrist, did you lose consciousness at any point, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. \n\nIt's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... \n\nBut yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school as a child and teenager, did you have trouble paying attention in class or staying focused on your schoolwork back then, or is this attention problem something that really only started around the time you broke your wrist?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, even way back in grade school, well before I was 12, my teachers were always telling my mother the same thing. They'd say I was bright, but just so careless. I was always losing my homework, or I'd forget the instructions right after they gave them, and I'd just sit there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. \n\nI remember in Mrs. Gable's fourth-grade class, my desk was right next to this big oak tree outside the window. We had these huge windows in that old school building—they don't make them like that anymore, all brick and those tall windows that you had to use a long wooden pole with a hook on the end just to pull the top sash down. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used to let me help him with the pole sometimes if I stayed after school. I mean, I scraped by. Barely passed some of them, especially math. I was always in the lowest math group, the one they put in the back corner of the room. We had these workbooks with the green covers, and I swear, just looking at that green color made me want to take a nap. \n\nGreen has always been a weird color for me anyway. Like, I love the color of grass and trees, obviously, but green clothes? Never look right on me. It washes out my complexion completely. My sister, though, she can wear emerald green and look like a movie star. She wore this stunning green velvet dress to our cousin's wedding back in... oh, it must have been '88 or '89, because they had that terrible DJ who kept playing Richard Marx songs. She spilled red wine down the front of it during the toast. Red wine on green velvet—it was a disaster. She tried using club soda and salt, which is what my grandmother always swore by, but it just left this weird crusty ring. Grandma had all these home remedies. She used to put butter on burns, which I guess they say now is the worst thing you can do...\n\nBut anyway, no, I didn't get held back. I just got a lot of C-minuses and notes sent home about needing to \"apply myself\" more.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides the wrist break, have you had any other significant illnesses, infections, head injuries, or medical problems in the past year or so—anything that required hospitalization or made you feel really unwell?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, nothing like that. It's really just been the wrist. I mean, I get the occasional cold, but nothing serious. I haven't been in the hospital for anything else. 

Hospitals are so freezing anyway, aren't they? When I was in the ER for my wrist, I was shivering the whole time. They gave me one of those heated blankets, which felt amazing for about five minutes before it went completely cold. I don't know why they can't just keep them plugged in or something. My aunt Shirley got me this electric throw blanket for Christmas last year, and it is the best thing ever. It's this really soft micro-fleece, kind of a slate blue color. Shirley always gives the most practical gifts. The year before that, she got me a set of silicone spatulas that I use almost every single day. They don't melt if you leave them leaning on the edge of the pan, which I'm terrible about doing. I ruined two good wooden spoons that way. Just left them right on the edge of the skillet while I was making chili, and the handles got all charred. My husband was so annoyed because they were part of a matching set we bought at this little craft fair up in Vermont. We went up there to see the leaves change, which is beautiful, but the traffic on Route 100 was just unbelievable...

So, no, no other medical issues. Just the broken wrist.

They put me on shop paperwork and supervising the younger crew instead of my usual hands-on carpentry. And let me tell you, sitting at a desk looking at invoices... I don't know how office people do it. My chair squeaks every single time I move, and I keep meaning to bring in some WD-40. I actually bought a can at the hardware store last Tuesday, but I keep leaving it in the cup holder of my truck... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The focus. Yeah, since that switch to the paperwork a year and a month ago, my brain just will not stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble focusing on your work, do you find yourself worrying a lot—like about things that might go wrong, or tasks you need to get done, or other concerns?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the roof caving in or whether the bank is going to call. My husband, Tom, now he's a worrier. He'll pace the kitchen floor for an hour if the electric bill is ten dollars higher than last month. I keep telling him it's just the space heater I put out in the garage for the stray cat that's been hanging around. Poor little orange thing, looks just like a cat I had when I was a kid named Marmalade. Marmalade used to sleep right on top of my dad's old Chevy... \n\nBut no, I'm not sitting at the desk worrying about things going wrong. It's more like my brain just... empties out, or fills up with a hundred different things that aren't the invoices. I'll be looking at a lumber order, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to water the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about how the neighbor's dog keeps digging under the fence, and before I know it, I'm looking up fence repair videos on YouTube. But I'm not anxious about it, you know? Just... completely sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that desk trying to work on the invoices, do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety—like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel like your body is just itching to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. If I have to sit at that desk doing estimates, or if they drag me into one of those long safety meetings... I just feel so wound up inside. Like I physically need to get up and move around. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\nDoctor: When you broke your wrist in that fall on the sawdust, did you hit your head at all, or did you have any other injuries besides the wrist?\n\nPatient: No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you fell on the sawdust and broke your wrist, did you lose consciousness at any point, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. \n\nIt's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... \n\nBut yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school as a child and teenager, did you have trouble paying attention in class or staying focused on your schoolwork back then, or is this attention problem something that really only started around the time you broke your wrist?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, even way back in grade school, well before I was 12, my teachers were always telling my mother the same thing. They'd say I was bright, but just so careless. I was always losing my homework, or I'd forget the instructions right after they gave them, and I'd just sit there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. \n\nI remember in Mrs. Gable's fourth-grade class, my desk was right next to this big oak tree outside the window. We had these huge windows in that old school building—they don't make them like that anymore, all brick and those tall windows that you had to use a long wooden pole with a hook on the end just to pull the top sash down. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used to let me help him with the pole sometimes if I stayed after school. He was such a sweet older man, always had a pocket full of those strawberry candies with the wrappers that looked like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even still make them. Tom's mom used to keep a glass dish of them on her coffee table, right next to this awful porcelain poodle collection... \n\nBut yeah, the attention stuff has been there my whole life. The wrist thing just made it so I have to sit at a desk more, which is why it's driving me so crazy now.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and high school, did your attention problems cause you to actually fail classes or get held back a grade, or were you able to pass despite the difficulties your teachers mentioned?\n\nPatient: Well, I never actually failed a grade, thank goodness. I mean, I scraped by. Barely passed some of them, especially math. I was always in the lowest math group, the one they put in the back corner of the room. We had these workbooks with the green covers, and I swear, just looking at that green color made me want to take a nap. \n\nGreen has always been a weird color for me anyway. Like, I love the color of grass and trees, obviously, but green clothes? Never look right on me. It washes out my complexion completely. My sister, though, she can wear emerald green and look like a movie star. She wore this stunning green velvet dress to our cousin's wedding back in... oh, it must have been '88 or '89, because they had that terrible DJ who kept playing Richard Marx songs. She spilled red wine down the front of it during the toast. Red wine on green velvet—it was a disaster. She tried using club soda and salt, which is what my grandmother always swore by, but it just left this weird crusty ring. Grandma had all these home remedies. She used to put butter on burns, which I guess they say now is the worst thing you can do...\n\nBut anyway, no, I didn't get held back. I just got a lot of C-minuses and notes sent home about needing to \"apply myself\" more.\n\nDoctor: Besides the wrist break, have you had any other significant illnesses, infections, head injuries, or medical problems in the past year or so—anything that required hospitalization or made you feel really unwell?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. It's really just been the wrist. I mean, I get the occasional cold, but nothing serious. I haven't been in the hospital for anything else. \n\nHospitals are so freezing anyway, aren't they? When I was in the ER for my wrist, I was shivering the whole time. They gave me one of those heated blankets, which felt amazing for about five minutes before it went completely cold. I don't know why they can't just keep them plugged in or something. My aunt Shirley got me this electric throw blanket for Christmas last year, and it is the best thing ever. It's this really soft micro-fleece, kind of a slate blue color. Shirley always gives the most practical gifts. The year before that, she got me a set of silicone spatulas that I use almost every single day. They don't melt if you leave them leaning on the edge of the pan, which I'm terrible about doing. I ruined two good wooden spoons that way. Just left them right on the edge of the skillet while I was making chili, and the handles got all charred. My husband was so annoyed because they were part of a matching set we bought at this little craft fair up in Vermont. We went up there to see the leaves change, which is beautiful, but the traffic on Route 100 was just unbelievable...\n\nSo, no, no other medical issues. Just the broken wrist.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Well, it was pretty much right away, honestly. I mean, the whole thing started about a year and a month ago when I broke it, right? And because I couldn't use my hand, they immediately pulled me off the actual carpentry work and stuck me in the office doing shop paperwork and supervising. And sitting at that desk all day... oh my gosh. My brain just turned to mush immediately. 

I'm used to being on my feet, you know? Having my hands on the wood, measuring, cutting. There's this one type of cedar we use for custom decks, Spanish cedar, and the smell of it when you run it through the planer is just incredible. It smells kind of like cigar boxes. My grandfather used to smoke these awful, cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called? He'd sit on the porch in this rickety old rocking chair that squeaked every time he moved. Squeak, squeak, squeak. It drove my grandmother absolutely up the wall. She tried spraying WD-40 on it once when he was asleep, but she missed the joint and got it all over the porch boards, and the mailman actually slipped on it the next day. He didn't break anything, thankfully, but he dropped all the letters in the mud. We got our electric bill that month and it had this big brown footprint right across the envelope...

But yeah, the trouble focusing really hit me hard as soon as they moved me to the paperwork. It's been like this for the whole year and a month since the accident. Just sitting there staring at invoices while my mind is a million miles away.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9, F06.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi Doctor. I guess what brings me in is... I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s getting really bad at the shop. Like, I’ll be in the middle of a project, and just one saw starting up in the background—we mostly use these Makita miter saws, though my buddy Dave swears by DeWalt, and he's always trying to get me to switch. I've had mine for ten years, you know, and it's never let me down. But anyway... if a saw starts up, or if somebody just walks past the office, or even if I just have a random thought about another job I need to do, it completely pulls me off what I’m doing. And then the whole day just gets away from me. Just yesterday I wasted hours because I thought about Mrs. Gable's cabinets, and she's a sweet lady, makes the absolute best snickerdoodles, but I wasn't even supposed to be working on her kitchen yet! I just... I can't stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing like this, or is this something that's gotten worse recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a little all over the place, I guess, but this specific level of trouble? That's definitely newer. It really ramped up about a year and a month ago. That was right around the time I broke my wrist. \n\nGosh, that was a whole ordeal. I was unloading some beautiful cherry wood—I was actually going to build a custom dining table for my niece's wedding. She had this gorgeous outdoor ceremony up in Colorado, though the altitude really did a number on my breathing. I had to sit down twice just walking to the reception tent! But anyway, I slipped on some sawdust in the shop and caught myself completely wrong. Snapped it right there. \n\nSo because I was in this bulky cast, they had to shift me off the tools. They put me on shop paperwork and supervising the younger crew instead of my usual hands-on carpentry. And let me tell you, sitting at a desk looking at invoices... I don't know how office people do it. My chair squeaks every single time I move, and I keep meaning to bring in some WD-40. I actually bought a can at the hardware store last Tuesday, but I keep leaving it in the cup holder of my truck... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The focus. Yeah, since that switch to the paperwork a year and a month ago, my brain just will not stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you're having trouble focusing on your work, do you find yourself worrying a lot—like about things that might go wrong, or tasks you need to get done, or other concerns?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier. It's not like I'm sitting there fretting about the roof caving in or whether the bank is going to call. My husband, Tom, now he's a worrier. He'll pace the kitchen floor for an hour if the electric bill is ten dollars higher than last month. I keep telling him it's just the space heater I put out in the garage for the stray cat that's been hanging around. Poor little orange thing, looks just like a cat I had when I was a kid named Marmalade. Marmalade used to sleep right on top of my dad's old Chevy... \n\nBut no, I'm not sitting at the desk worrying about things going wrong. It's more like my brain just... empties out, or fills up with a hundred different things that aren't the invoices. I'll be looking at a lumber order, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to water the tomatoes, and then I'm thinking about how the neighbor's dog keeps digging under the fence, and before I know it, I'm looking up fence repair videos on YouTube. But I'm not anxious about it, you know? Just... completely sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at that desk trying to work on the invoices, do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety—like you need to get up and move around, or do you feel like your body is just itching to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, absolutely. If I have to sit at that desk doing estimates, or if they drag me into one of those long safety meetings... I just feel so wound up inside. Like I physically need to get up and move around. It's this awful crawling feeling. Even though my wrist is totally healed now and I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish the paperwork, I just can't stand being still. \n\nIt reminds me of when I was a kid and my mom would make me sit through Sunday service. We went to this little Methodist church down on 4th Street, the one with the really beautiful stained glass windows? The colors were just gorgeous when the morning sun hit them, especially the blues. I always wanted to learn how to do stained glass. I actually looked into taking a class at the community college a few years back, but the instructor moved away to Portland. I hear Portland's nice, lots of rain though. Tom's sister lives out there and she complains about the dampness ruining her garden... \n\nBut yeah, sitting at the desk, I just feel like I'm going to burst out of my skin if I don't get up and walk around the shop.\n\nDoctor: When you broke your wrist in that fall on the sawdust, did you hit your head at all, or did you have any other injuries besides the wrist?\n\nPatient: No, no, just the wrist. I caught myself on my hand, so my head never hit the ground. Thank goodness for that, right? I mean, my hard hat was sitting on the workbench anyway. I always take it off when I'm doing the fine sanding because the brim gets in the way of my line of sight. It's a really nice hard hat, actually—one of those carbon fiber ones? The crew bought it for my 50th birthday because I used to complain about how heavy the standard plastic ones were, giving me neck aches. \n\nNeck aches are the worst. I tried this special ergonomic pillow for a while, the kind with the little dip in the middle for your head? But it just made me sleep weird and I'd wake up with my ear completely numb. Tom thought it was hilarious, but he can sleep on an airplane, so what does he know? He actually fell asleep during takeoff once when we went to Florida. Florida was so humid, my hair just completely gave up... \n\nBut yeah, no head injury. Just the wrist. Snapped it good, but my head was perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you fell on the sawdust and broke your wrist, did you lose consciousness at any point, even for just a few seconds?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't pass out or anything like that. I was wide awake the whole time. I remember just lying there on my back in the sawdust, holding my arm and staring up at the ceiling, and noticing that one of the fluorescent light tubes was flickering. I had been meaning to change that bulb for weeks. \n\nIt's one of those really long tubes, you know? They're such a pain to get rid of because you can't just throw them in the regular shop dumpster—I think they have mercury or something in them. Tom usually takes them down to the county recycling center on Saturday mornings. He actually loves going to the dump, if you can believe it. It's like a social event for him. He always runs into guys from his bowling league and they stand around drinking coffee out of a thermos by the cardboard bins... \n\nBut yeah, no, I didn't lose consciousness at all. I just laid there watching that stupid light blink until one of the guys heard me yelling.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school as a child and teenager, did you have trouble paying attention in class or staying focused on your schoolwork back then, or is this attention problem something that really only started around the time you broke your wrist?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not a new thing. I mean, even way back in grade school, well before I was 12, my teachers were always telling my mother the same thing. They'd say I was bright, but just so careless. I was always losing my homework, or I'd forget the instructions right after they gave them, and I'd just sit there staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. \n\nI remember in Mrs. Gable's fourth-grade class, my desk was right next to this big oak tree outside the window. We had these huge windows in that old school building—they don't make them like that anymore, all brick and those tall windows that you had to use a long wooden pole with a hook on the end just to pull the top sash down. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used to let me help him with the pole sometimes if I stayed after school. He was such a sweet older man, always had a pocket full of those strawberry candies with the wrappers that looked like actual strawberries? I haven't seen those candies in years. I wonder if they even still make them. Tom's mom used to keep a glass dish of them on her coffee table, right next to this awful porcelain poodle collection... \n\nBut yeah, the attention stuff has been there my whole life. The wrist thing just made it so I have to sit at a desk more, which is why it's driving me so crazy now.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and high school, did your attention problems cause you to actually fail classes or get held back a grade, or were you able to pass despite the difficulties your teachers mentioned?\n\nPatient: Well, I never actually failed a grade, thank goodness. I mean, I scraped by. Barely passed some of them, especially math. I was always in the lowest math group, the one they put in the back corner of the room. We had these workbooks with the green covers, and I swear, just looking at that green color made me want to take a nap. \n\nGreen has always been a weird color for me anyway. Like, I love the color of grass and trees, obviously, but green clothes? Never look right on me. It washes out my complexion completely. My sister, though, she can wear emerald green and look like a movie star. She wore this stunning green velvet dress to our cousin's wedding back in... oh, it must have been '88 or '89, because they had that terrible DJ who kept playing Richard Marx songs. She spilled red wine down the front of it during the toast. Red wine on green velvet—it was a disaster. She tried using club soda and salt, which is what my grandmother always swore by, but it just left this weird crusty ring. Grandma had all these home remedies. She used to put butter on burns, which I guess they say now is the worst thing you can do...\n\nBut anyway, no, I didn't get held back. I just got a lot of C-minuses and notes sent home about needing to \"apply myself\" more.\n\nDoctor: Besides the wrist break, have you had any other significant illnesses, infections, head injuries, or medical problems in the past year or so—anything that required hospitalization or made you feel really unwell?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. It's really just been the wrist. I mean, I get the occasional cold, but nothing serious. I haven't been in the hospital for anything else. \n\nHospitals are so freezing anyway, aren't they? When I was in the ER for my wrist, I was shivering the whole time. They gave me one of those heated blankets, which felt amazing for about five minutes before it went completely cold. I don't know why they can't just keep them plugged in or something. My aunt Shirley got me this electric throw blanket for Christmas last year, and it is the best thing ever. It's this really soft micro-fleece, kind of a slate blue color. Shirley always gives the most practical gifts. The year before that, she got me a set of silicone spatulas that I use almost every single day. They don't melt if you leave them leaning on the edge of the pan, which I'm terrible about doing. I ruined two good wooden spoons that way. Just left them right on the edge of the skillet while I was making chili, and the handles got all charred. My husband was so annoyed because they were part of a matching set we bought at this little craft fair up in Vermont. We went up there to see the leaves change, which is beautiful, but the traffic on Route 100 was just unbelievable...\n\nSo, no, no other medical issues. Just the broken wrist.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the attention problems ramped up about a year and a month ago when you broke your wrist. But when you first got injured and were in that cast—in those first few weeks—did your focus problems feel noticeably worse right away, or did they gradually get worse over months as you spent more time doing desk work?\n\nPatient: Well, it was pretty much right away, honestly. I mean, the whole thing started about a year and a month ago when I broke it, right? And because I couldn't use my hand, they immediately pulled me off the actual carpentry work and stuck me in the office doing shop paperwork and supervising. And sitting at that desk all day... oh my gosh. My brain just turned to mush immediately. \n\nI'm used to being on my feet, you know? Having my hands on the wood, measuring, cutting. There's this one type of cedar we use for custom decks, Spanish cedar, and the smell of it when you run it through the planer is just incredible. It smells kind of like cigar boxes. My grandfather used to smoke these awful, cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called? He'd sit on the porch in this rickety old rocking chair that squeaked every time he moved. Squeak, squeak, squeak. It drove my grandmother absolutely up the wall. She tried spraying WD-40 on it once when he was asleep, but she missed the joint and got it all over the porch boards, and the mailman actually slipped on it the next day. He didn't break anything, thankfully, but he dropped all the letters in the mud. We got our electric bill that month and it had this big brown footprint right across the envelope...\n\nBut yeah, the trouble focusing really hit me hard as soon as they moved me to the paperwork. It's been like this for the whole year and a month since the accident. Just sitting there staring at invoices while my mind is a million miles away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's really just been the wrist. I mean, I get the occasional cold, but nothing serious. I haven't been in the hospital for anything else. \n\nHospitals are so freezing anyway, aren't they? When I was in the ER for my wrist, I was shivering the whole time. They gave me one of those heated blankets, which felt amazing for about five minutes before it went completely cold. I don't know why they can't just keep them plugged in or something. My aunt Shirley got me this electric throw blanket for Christmas last year, and it is the best thing ever. It's this really soft micro-fleece, kind of a slate blue color. Shirley always gives the most practical gifts. The year before that, she got me a set of silicone spatulas that I use almost every single day. They don't melt if you leave them leaning on the edge of the pan, which I'm terrible about doing. My grandfather used to smoke these awful, cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called? He'd sit on the porch in this rickety old rocking chair that squeaked every time he moved. It's been like this for the whole year and a month since the accident. Just sitting there staring at invoices while my mind is a million miles away.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore you broke your wrist a year and a month ago, when you were doing your regular carpentry work—hands-on with the tools and projects—were you able to focus and stay on task pretty well, or were you still having significant trouble keeping your mind on what you were doing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, honestly. Even before the accident, when I was right in the middle of a job, I was still drifting off. I mean, I love the work, but I'd be measuring a board and suddenly I'm thinking about... I don't know, whether I left the kitchen window open at home. And then I'd have to measure it three more times just to be sure, because I'd completely forget the number the second I looked away from the tape measure. 

Tape measures are funny things, aren't they? I have this one Stanley tape measure that I've had for probably fifteen years. It's got this dent on the side from when it fell off a roof we were doing in, oh, I want to say 2010? It was a really steep pitch, and the homeowner had these gorgeous hydrangeas planted right below. Huge, blue blooms. I always try to grow hydrangeas, but mine never turn blue. They just stay this washed-out pink color. I read somewhere that you have to put coffee grounds or rusty nails in the soil to change the acidity, but I can never remember to save my coffee grounds. I use a French press, and cleaning it out is such a chore. The grounds get stuck in the little mesh filter, and if you rinse them down the sink, it clogs the pipes. My husband had to take the P-trap apart last Thanksgiving because the sink wouldn't drain, right when I was trying to peel potatoes for twelve people...

But anyway, yeah, even when I was doing the hands-on stuff, my mind was always wandering off like that. It just wasn't quite as bad as sitting at a desk all day.

Squeak, squeak, squeak. It drove my grandmother absolutely up the wall. She tried spraying WD-40 on it once when he was asleep, but she missed the joint and got it all over the porch boards, and the mailman actually slipped on it the next day. He didn't break anything, thankfully, but he dropped all the letters in the mud. We got our electric bill that month and it had this big brown footprint right across the envelope...\n\nBut yeah, the trouble focusing really hit me hard as soon as they moved me to the paperwork. It's been like this for the whole year and a month since the accident. Just sitting there staring at invoices while my mind is a million miles away.\n\nDoctor: Before you broke your wrist a year and a month ago, when you were doing your regular carpentry work—hands-on with the tools and projects—were you able to focus and stay on task pretty well, or were you still having significant trouble keeping your mind on what you were doing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, honestly. Even before the accident, when I was right in the middle of a job, I was still drifting off. I mean, I love the work, but I'd be measuring a board and suddenly I'm thinking about... I don't know, whether I left the kitchen window open at home. I have this one Stanley tape measure that I've had for probably fifteen years. It's got this dent on the side from when it fell off a roof we were doing in, oh, I want to say 2010? It was a really steep pitch, and the homeowner had these gorgeous hydrangeas planted right below. Huge, blue blooms. I always try to grow hydrangeas, but mine never turn blue. They just stay this washed-out pink color. I read somewhere that you have to put coffee grounds or rusty nails in the soil to change the acidity, but I can never remember to save my coffee grounds. I use a French press, and cleaning it out is such a chore. The grounds get stuck in the little mesh filter, and if you rinse them down the sink, it clogs the pipes. My husband had to take the P-trap apart last Thanksgiving because the sink wouldn't drain, right when I was trying to peel potatoes for twelve people...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, even when I was doing the hands-on stuff, my mind was always wandering off like that. It just wasn't quite as bad as sitting at a desk all day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it was definitely there back in grade school, way before I was 12. My teachers were always telling my mother at parent-teacher conferences that I was a bright girl, but just so careless. I was constantly losing my homework, forgetting the instructions the second they were given, and just staring out the window instead of finishing my seatwork. 

I used to watch this huge oak tree right outside Mrs. Gable's third-grade classroom. There were always these gray squirrels chasing each other round and round the trunk. Squirrels are so bold nowadays, aren't they? We have one that comes right up to our back porch and absolutely robs the bird feeder. My husband bought this supposedly "squirrel-proof" feeder from the hardware store last spring. It has this spring-loaded metal ring that's supposed to pull down and block the seed when something heavy sits on it. Well, the squirrels figured out that if they hang upside down from the rain gutter, they can just bypass the ring entirely and scoop the sunflower seeds out with their little paws. They are just incredibly smart. 

It actually reminds me of this documentary I saw on PBS about crows. Did you know crows can remember human faces? If you're mean to a crow, it'll tell all its crow friends, and they'll literally hold a grudge against you for years. I always try to leave a little bit of unsalted peanut out on the railing for them, just to stay on their good side. But then if you leave food out, you get ants. We had these tiny little sugar ants in the kitchen all summer, marching right along the baseboards. I tried using that peppermint oil spray because I didn't want to use harsh chemicals near where we eat, but it just made the kitchen smell like a candy cane and didn't stop the ants at all...
